@@138780376 <h> Jewish youth programming sees a post-Oct. 7 boom across Canada <p> Students at a Jewish Students Union club at Sir Robert Borden high school in Ottawa participate in a Lego challenge . ( Photo courtesy of NCSY ) <p> When Ottawa opened its new BBYO chapter in December 2025 , Leora Taub-Handelman , 15 , was one of the inaugural members . As the vice-president of programming , one of 10 leadership roles at the new chapter , she helped their membership grow to around 30 people by the end of the academic year . <p> " It 's definitely very satisfying , " Taub-Handelman says . " It 's a place where you can connect with people with very low pressure , low stakes , but very high reward . You can just go have fun , does n't matter what your background is . " <p> The small Jewish population in Ottawa made it difficult for Taub-Handelman to find a welcoming , non-denominational space before the last school year . When the BBYO chapter opened , she only had two to three volunteers to run events , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on successful programs from Hamilton 's BBYO chapter , which opened in 2022 . <p> When the 2026-27 academic year starts , Taub-Handelman and the board plan to set a consistent schedule of events every second week . <p> " It 's nice to know that you 're actually making a difference and people can enjoy it , " she says . <p> Taub-Handelman 's Ottawa chapter is one of 16 that have opened across the country since the COVID-19 pandemic . And BBYO is n't the only Jewish youth organization that has seen a surge in membership from teens looking to discover more about their Jewish identities : over the last five years , NFTY , the youth group for the Reform movement , has similarly expanded its programming to Canadian cities with smaller Jewish populations , and NCSY Canada , the Orthodox movement 's youth group , has opened more than 20 public school clubs . <p> BBYO nearly doubles its chapters across Canada <p> Kevin Goodman is the executive director of BBYO Eastern Canada . He says BBYO 's 16 new chapters have nearly doubled the organization @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ an incredible surge in BBYO participation throughout COVID , and of course following October 7 , " Goodman says . Following the Oct. 7 , 2023 , Hamas attack on Israel and the subsequent Gaza-Israel war and the sharp rise of antisemitism in Canada , Jewish students turned to the non-denominational youth group . " People were seeking out the opportunities that BBYO offers : community , connection , comfort , Jewish engagement . " <p> New BBYO spaces include LGBTQ-focused and Hebrew-speaking chapters in the Toronto area ; five chapters in B.C. ; four chapters in the Alberta-Saskatchewan region ; and chapters in communities with smaller Jewish populations , such as Ottawa and Hamilton . <p> Many of these chapters have opened because students and parents in the area reached out to BBYO , Goodman says . Around once a week , he gets inquiries from other areas without existing chapters , including in Kingston , London , Newmarket , Barrie , and downtown Toronto . <p> " If I could flick a magic switch and launch in all of those communities tomorrow , I would , but we @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ communities strategically , " Goodman says . " We 're limited only in our bandwidth -- our human resources and financial resources -- to ensure that what we 're building is sustainable for the long term . " <p> He adds that the chapters and their activities are " easy to replicate " because they 're led by the students , with the help of alumni who act as volunteer advisors . Chapters operate on an average of $10,000 to $20,000 a year , Goodman says , with basic program supplies and staffing making up most expenses . <p> Most BBYO chapters have at most 80 members once they 're established in the community , Goodman says . Some of the newer chapters are just reaching 30 to 40 members , including the LGBTQ-focused Simcha BBYO that opened in January 2023 and the Hebrew-speaking BBYO B'Ivrit , established in fall 2024 . <p> " You see the full-fledged scope of a BBYO chapter four or five years in , " Goodman says , adding that memberships continue from Grade 8 until high school graduation . <p> Reform organization grows social @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of team strategy for the Reform movement 's North American Federation for Temple Youth ( NFTY ) . This year 's NFTY events have been attended by more than 100 participants . <p> Margolis says Jews are taking part in youth programs more often because it improves their well-being . They credit Nyala , an Ethiopian restaurant on Main Street that closed up shop in 2011 , with helping them to explore their creative side while they were studying at UBC . <p> " To stay sane going through university , I had to have a life outside classes , " they recall . " Every Tuesday , Nyala would have open mics , and that was my first exposure to spoken word in the city , and acoustic singer-songwriters and rappers . I would use the journal entries that I wrote and turn them into songs , so I would sing and rap on my guitar . " <p> Those Lauryn Hill -- style early jams were where Kimmortal first established their highly personal lyrical voice . The period also heavily influenced their almost-forgotten first album , Sincerity , which dropped in 2014 . <p> " It was n't totally a reflection of what I was listening to , which was more like electronic and hip-hop , " they admit . <p> So they took a class to learn how @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to 2019 's full-length follow-up , X Marks the Swirl . <p> " I would sample like , the sounds of water for that album , which was really based on water , and more experimental , " they add . " I still play a lot of those songs today : ' Sad Femme Club ' is on that , ' I 'm Blue ' is from that album . That was a very formative album . " <p> It was also around that time that Kimmortal leaned more into the other artistic aspects of creativity . <p> " I really got into visuals , like , ' What is the world I 'm creating ? ' " they say . " That 's the album where I was really like , ' I can be anything I want to be : I can really create the world of Kimmortal and create a portal . ' " <p> That cohesiveness is still apparent whenever Kimmortal takes the stage . They take great pains to create a distinctive look on stage with their clothes and style and perform with their @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ performer after graduation , too , and that sense of showmanship ( or uh , showpersonship ? ) still informs their musical performance style . <p> " For seven years after I graduated , I was in this hip-hop theatre production , and I acted as a 16-year-old Filipina MC battle rapper , " Kimmortal says with a laugh . " I love theatre because of the embodiment , so I bring that to the stage . I really love to put myself through psychosomatic journeys , and also hopefully support people in the audience in , like , psychosomatic shit . One of the most formative workshops I was ever in was an ancestral embodiment workshop through Urban Ink . That was back in like 2012 and it changed my entire performance life . " <p> Kimmortal makes music for the diaspora , rooted in Filipino practice but relatable to anyone trying to untangle histories of colonization , repression , or dislocation . <p> The workshop , they explain , involved trance , writing , and attempting to embody an ancestor from at least three generations ago . For @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Pangasinan and Negros Occidental ancestry , connecting to their familial past helped them understand the power of telling stories to an audience . <p> " What I took away from it was how deep performance can go , " they reflect . " It 's kind of a wild thing to witness a body onstage performing a song to you , and for me , it 's a very personal thing for me to drop into a song and then to tell the story with my body and not just my voice . " <p> Yvonne Hanson <p> But the flip side of X Marks the Swirl 's success was the endless hard work that being an independent musician requires . Kimmortal burnt out , and , for all its horrors , the pandemic offered an opportunity to start over . Rest , reset , and recentre . <p> The 2023 full-length Shoebox reflects that period of the singer 's life , when they were getting used to spending time in their own head and reconnecting with their inner child . <p> " Me and my partner moved to Port @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , so I feel like Shoebox took place in the forest , " Kimmortal reflects . " I was trying to unlearn patterns of productivity in my art , so I was scribbling , taking art therapy classes , really trying to heal , and working through old wounds . " <p> While there are some certified bangers on the album -- " This Dyke " is a horny , motor-revving lust song that got featured on The L Word : Generation Q , while " Do n't Be Afraid " is a tight danceable track about taking up space -- Kimmortal says their favourite track on the album is " Run " , a deeply personal narrative rap about the darkness we inherit from our families . <p> " That song makes me cry , " Kimmortal says . " I wrote that song 10 years before I released it . It talks about intergenerational trauma and mental illness , and the whole album 's about mental illness . But that song needed a container , and Shoebox was a perfect container . " <p> Since then , Kimmortal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sound , hardly static in the first place , has evolved and changed even further . " Stop Business As Usual " served as an important inflection point : a song released in November 2023 , written in the immediate aftermath of Israel 's heavy-handed response to the October 7 terrorist attack . It became arguably one of the first protest songs to explicitly comment on the moment , six months before Macklemore released " HIND 'S HALL " . <p> Although in the years since there has come to be more mainstream acceptance that Israel 's military actions in Gaza do count as genocide , at the time , it was a very explosive opinion . " I do n't wan na write a record , I wan na reflect the times , " Kimmortal raps over a sparse , almost dreamy beat . " Stop business as usual , stop the genocide . " <p> " ? ' Stop Business As Usual ' was played on a freedom flotilla to Gaza . Holy shit , " Kimmortal recalls , awe in their voice . <p> The track means @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of spreading a message , but as a method of connecting people . <p> " Someone I met in Germany , a queer Asian femme , told me that ' Stop Business As Usual ' helped during the time they were losing friends and making new friends during the pro-Palestine movement , " Kimmortal says . " They said , ' You do n't even know how much your song gave me strength and got me through that time . ' ? " <p> A 2024 reworking of the song , " Stop Business As Usual ( Part 2 ) " sees Toronto MC pHoenix Pagliacci and Ayachuco-American poet Bobby Sanchez hop on the track to drop their own 16-bar verses . " Swoletarian " is a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ decidedly unserious take on the dark problem of rising authoritarianism : a bouncy workout jam with echoing vocals , an infectious guitar riff , and driving percussion . <p> The lyrics are where the song shines : Kimmortal spits through the opening bars of " I 'm a swoletarian/Read you like a librarian/Flip all the contrarians/Squash beef like vegetarians " with an easy braggadocio before declaring they 're " training for the army of the U-S-Gay " . <p> " SuperKnova was like , ' What are you thinking about these days ? ' And at the time , my partner Tin Lorica was working out -- all of a sudden they were doing reps , " Kimmortal recalls . " And then contextually the rise of fascism , talking about building muscle with friends -- we 're reclaiming that shit outside of fatphobic shit , outside of ' do it to get skinny ' , it 's like , ' do it to get buff and punch a fascist ' . " <p> The song is sure to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ summer of playing various Pride festivals across B.C. , they are excited to play an outdoor festival in their hometown , with some accompaniment from Apex Twink ( one half of experimental Pinoy duo Gulod Tanawin ) . <p> Kimmortal has performed at Khatsahlano before , pre-pandemic . <p> " I remember I came up wearing a cape -- I just went to Fabricland and bought a blue cape and wore it on stage , " Kimmortal says . @@138892515 <p> Divers place mock munitions on the floor of the Mediterranean Sea off the coast of Rishon LeZion , Israel , Sunday , June 21 , 2026 . ( AP Photo/Sam Mednick ) <p> RISHON LEZION , Israel ( AP ) -- Marking the coordinates on a handheld GPS , an Israeli diver threw an anchor into the water as another quickly chucked an orange buoy beside it . Cramped on the boat 's bow , the first team assembled their gear , put on wet suits and tested oxygen tanks before jumping in . <p> But after hours of combing the Mediterranean seabed in search of yellow-painted mock mortar shells , the divers surfaced empty-handed . <p> It was the team 's fifth diving trip in the yearslong experiment to help prepare Israel to clear part of the sea from unexploded grenades and other munitions in order to return beach area to residents . But on this day in June , the divers could n't find the dummy mortar and artillery shells they 'd planted months prior , foreshadowing the challenges that lie ahead . <p> " It 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ said Roy Jaijel , a researcher in the marine geology and geophysics department at Israel 's National Institute of Oceanography , as he emerged from a dive . <p> Jaijel co-leads a project aimed at returning some 2 kilometers ( 1.2 miles ) of shoreline to people living in Israel 's central city of Rishon LeZion , an area that 's been used as a firing range for decades . The initiative , the first of its kind in Israel , coincides with a global push to better protect the world 's waters as demand increases for the use of seas and oceans for shipping , energy and recreation . <p> Experts say the clearance of underwater munitions has received more attention in recent years in part because of the boom in artificial intelligence , which requires millions of kilometers of underwater fiber-optic cables to allow for global connectivity . <p> Munitions can end up dumped into waters after wars , fall into seas during conflict or , in the case of Rishon LeZion , accumulate from firing practice . Erosion from seawater can lead toxic and explosive chemicals , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , causing environmental contamination . There 's also the risk of objects exploding if people step on them or children play with them , thinking they 're toys . <p> Two years ago , Europe launched a project to better detect and clear non-military unexploded ordnance , such as from industrial or commercial sites . In a separate initiative in 2024 , Germany piloted a program to recover and dispose of military waste from the North and Baltic seas , where some 1.6 million tonnes of unexploded munitions from two world wars lie , according to the German government . <p> Still , there 's been less focus on clearing waters in the Middle East , such as the Mediterranean , which historically has n't been the site of large dumps compared with Europe . <p> Leaders of the Israeli project say it 's one of the first to focus on clearing smaller munitions in complicated underwater terrain , which is why many countries have avoided it . <p> " It 's like looking for a needle in a haystack , " said Israel Faintuch , head of the Maritime @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Authority as he checked his oxygen tank and suited up to go underwater . <p> Limited beach space in Israel is the driving force behind the clearing effort <p> The government says nearly half the country 's 194-kilometer ( 120-mile ) coastline is off limits to civilians , used for commercial ports , power plants , desalination facilities , military bases and firing zones . <p> Since the country 's founding nearly 80 years ago , 7 kilometers ( 4.3 miles ) , nearly the entire length of Rishon LeZion 's shoreline , has been used as a firing range , launching grenades as well as small and large mortars , leaving hundreds of thousands of people crammed into a narrow strip of beach . <p> Launched last year , the joint research project funded by Rishon LeZion 's municipality is being led by Israel 's National Mine Action Authority and researchers from the National Institute of Oceanography . It aims to localize the most impacted areas , mapping the pattern of munitions to determine how far offshore and how deep to go before the clearance team steps in . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sizes of fake munitions -- some equipped with motion sensors -- at depths of 5 , 10 and 15 meters ( 16 , 33 and 59 feet ) and up to 1.2 kilometers ( 0.75 miles ) offshore . After several months , they retrieve the munitions , analyze the data and plant new ones . <p> In June , Associated Press journalists accompanied the team underwater as they placed new munitions for the next round of tests and attempted to find ones they 'd left in January . Divers descended using a string , or measuring tape , to navigate the seabed . Tapping each other under the water , they 'd point in different directions to search , rubbing their hands over the seafloor . <p> " You have limited air supply when you go with the divers and you have limited time in the water , " said Dafna Eliahu , a graduate student at the University of Haifa working on the project . " So with actual live munition I expect it to be very difficult , very hard to locate and to actually be able @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the information , including from the sensors , is still being processed , preliminary findings show that the munitions moved less than expected , which means there might be less area that needs clearing , she said . <p> Israel 's Defense Ministry wants to have enough data to start clearing by the end of next year and expand the shoreline by an initial 150 meters ( 492 feet ) within a few months . Completing the project will take years and cost tens of millions of dollars . It 's already been delayed due to Israel 's multiple wars with Hamas in Gaza , Hezbollah in Lebanon and Iran as divers ca n't work when missiles are falling and could land in the sea . <p> During the current war that the U.S. and Israel launched against Iran as well as the 12-day war last June between Israel and Iran , the army said missiles aimed at larger cities like Rishon LeZion fell into the sea but would n't specify how many . <p> Israel says no one has been injured or killed by unexploded sea ordnance , but @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the last 20 years where the police and army were called . Most have been found on or near shore . <p> What 's learned during the project could be useful beyond Israel <p> While the goal of the project is to expand parts of the shoreline , Israel also hopes its findings will yield new insights on clearing munitions from this part of the world , where there are threats but overall less is known . <p> According to the Geneva International Centre for Humanitarian Demining , more than half of global incidents related to unexploded ordnance , such as sightings or drifting mines , were recorded in the Middle East between 2014 and 2023 , with most occurring in the Red Sea off the coast of Yemen and the Bab-el-Mandeb Strait , largely a result of Yemen 's civil war . <p> Pedro Basto , research and innovation program manager with the group , said it is important to keep interest high in removing underwater explosives given the increasing dependence on the seas . <p> " Both renewable energies based on the sea ( wind turbines and harnessing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the world relies on every minute of every day , depend massively on underwater cable laying , " he said . <p> As Israel 's project advances , residents in Rishon LeZion say they 're looking forward to being able to use more land . <p> Moria Malka , head spokesperson for the city 's municipality , said the clearance will triple the area 's coastline and much of it will become a nature reserve as well as a residential area near the sea . For beachgoers like Mark Kostman , that is great news . <p> " Holidays and Saturdays , all of this place is completely crowded and too dense to even have fun , " said Kostman as he played volleyball with his children next to the firing zone . " Having it as public space for leisure and sport ... it 's wonderful . " <p> The Associated Press ' climate and environmental coverage receives financial support from multiple private foundations . Today , voters are yearning for stability and real solutions , the exact opposite of what divisive populist politics promise . <p> THE CANADIAN PRESS / Jeff McIntosh <p> Conservative Leader Pierre Poilievre does n't seem to realize that to win , political leaders sometimes have to change ideological horses . And the ideology of rage politics is failing . <p> Events , current and past , rightly fuelled the anger . The 2008 financial crisis marked the beginning of our current " end times . " It was followed in short order by the first triumph of Donald Trump 's Make America Great Again movement in 2016 , Brexit in Britain in 2016 , the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020 , Russia 's invasion of Ukraine in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in Israel and that country 's two-year invasion and war in Gaza , and the triumphant return of Trump and MAGA in 2024 . Now comes the ongoing war with Iran launched by the U.S. and Israel . <p> Individually and in combination , these events roiled economies and society . Public institutions in Canada , Britain and America all managed ably enough through the crises but had insufficient imagination and ambition to anticipate and address the ensuing economic and social fallout . Public trust in government plummeted despite record levels of public spending . From homes to groceries to wages , nothing seemed affordable or attainable . <p> If there ever was a time for populist gains , this was it . And gain they did . Until they stopped . Brexit would not win in a U.K. referendum today . Trump is deeply unpopular with everyone but his MAGA base . His Republican party will struggle to win Congress in the November mid-term elections . Pierre Poilievre 's Conservatives lost a sure-thing election and are further from , not closer to power than in the past decade . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Trump 's government has bungled file after file , from DOGE cuts by Elon Musk to ICE raids by Kristi Noem to tariff hikes by Howard Lutnick . Each initiative was either bungled or reversed in some fashion after their high-profile and ill-conceived launches . <p> In Britain , revolving door prime ministers killed the brand of its most successful political party , the Conservatives . The current Labour government is showing the door to its first prime minister in 15 years , not halfway through its majority mandate . <p> Meanwhile , the poll-leading , populist upstart Reform UK party is imploding as its leader , Brexit-architect Nigel Farage , has resigned his seat in Parliament over allegations of concealing millions of dollars in personal donations . He plans to re-contest it as a classic two-fingers " up yours " to " the British establishment " to rekindle the rage flame of populism that is his brand . We 'll see . <p> Truth be told , populist leaders and movements just are n't made for the serious work of governing . Pierre Poilievre has a serious mien and takes himself very seriously . Mark Carney is a serious prime minister who does not . The image contrast is vivid . <p> More troubling for Poilievre is that he has allowed himself to become defined and , hence , trapped by the angry populist politics of the conservative movement . <p> Movements are not the same as parties . @@139009830 <p> Rabbi Yael Splansky , the senior leader of Toronto 's Holy Blossom Temple , presides over the Reform movement 's flagship institution and the oldest synagogue in the city at a pivotal time . <p> The temple , which is marking its 170th anniversary this year , has not only completed a major physical renewal on its Bathurst Street property , it is also in the midst of a membership boom , as Jews seek connection in an increasingly challenging post-October 7 world . <p> About 30 per cent of Holy Blossom 's 2,000 member households are new in the last five years , a staggering rate of growth relative to many modern Canadian congregations struggling to keep membership levels consistent . Rabbi Splansky refers to the phenomenon as " The Surge " in an interview with The CJN , where she discussed the temple 's significant milestone anniversary as well as its future . <p> Originally from Boston , Rabbi Splansky notes " That 's not typical across North America , so I 'm not taking it for granted by any stretch . " Holy Blossom , she @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are in the aftermath of October 7th , looking for ways to connect meaningfully and joyfully to Jewish life , and we 're able to provide that . " <p> There 's data showing " The Surge " is real . <p> " Service attendance is up , interest in ritual life is up . Membership is up , conversions are up . Donations are up . So , any marker that you can measure by , " amounts to a thriving Holy Blossom community , she explained . <p> Rabbi Splansky says people finding the congregation seek to be " anchored in tradition , " along with innovation . <p> " People want very much to tap into what is old and lasting and enduring and forever . They want to also tap into our history of survival , because God knows we 've seen rougher , tougher days than these . So , how did previous generations at Holy Blossom and beyond respond to external threats and internal threats , for that matter ? " <p> She added : " And then , people are also looking for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " <p> The congregation provides something for newcomers and longtime , loyal members , she says . <p> " We 're going to study our ancient texts . We 're going to turn to Holy Blossom history and retell those stories and tap into that wisdom and that courage about how to face the future with hope , and how not to let the haters win , " she said . " And it seems to be exactly what people need right now . " <p> Rabbi Splansky , who is a fourth-generation rabbi , became Holy Blossom 's thirteenth senior rabbi , and the first woman to hold that position , in 2014 . <p> Holy Blossom was the first Canadian synagogue to formally link with the Reform movement in 1920 , and the first Canadian congregation to hire women rabbis , beginning in 1980 . <p> Among its many significant leaders was the late Rabbi Gunther Plaut who served as senior rabbi from 1961 to 1977 , then became its first senior scholar . The internationally renowned Rabbi Plaut published prolifically , including The Torah : A Modern @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ disconnect over Israel <p> Holy Blossom 's leadership and engagement role became apparent once more last month , when the synagogue hosted Prime Minister Mark Carney for a high-profile address to and about the Jewish community . <p> Rabbi Splansky , who had been away , offered a video address that set the tone for Carney 's remarks , in which she specifically implored the prime minister to acknowledge the level to which the crisis involved " lovers of Israel " being targeted throughout society . <p> Carney 's words were strong and serious at first , she says . <p> " He was telling the story of antisemitism in this country and not pretending it away . Now it 's on the record . He is on the hook . The highest office in the land has said ' this is a national crisis , ' and he gave charge to every citizen to make this your concern . So that is not nothing , " she said . <p> " I think that is significant , and it needs to be leveraged now in every way that it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ play in that : synagogues , rabbis , lay leaders have roles to play in this ; schools and summer camps have roles to play , and certainly the organizations who have the mandate for civic engagement and advocacy , they now have new leverage . I think that was a win for us . " <p> The second half of the address , when the prime minister presented his " action items , " was another story . <p> " That 's clearly where things fell down , and we have to help him , and we have to help everyone else to get it right . " Rabbi Splansky acknowledges that wo n't be easy , but says " now we have something more to work with . " <p> Several leaders of Jewish organizations have criticized Carney 's speech for failing to specifically name the role anti-Israel sentiment has played in the current wave of antisemitism . <p> A past president of the Toronto Board of Rabbis , Rabbi Splansky says she maintains contact with elected officials at all levels . But an improved understanding among Canadians @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ government relations , she emphasizes . <p> " We have to insist that the Canadian government pursues a better working relationship with the government of Israel , " she said . " The relationship between the Zionism that most Canadian Jews hold dear is not understood by most Canadians and there 's work to be done , " she said . <p> While Holy Blossom did obtain a " bubble " zone designation from the City of Toronto mandating protests to keep away by 50 metres , the temple also put its own , more welcoming signs up to complement the less friendly printed notices the city requires . Earlier in 2026 , after three of the city-mandated signs were cut down from the shul 's fence , red paper hearts and notes of support appeared affixed to the doors the next morning . <p> " I feel that any synagogue , when asked : ' The prime minister of the country wants to address the crisis , the national crisis of antisemitism , ' the only answer is ' yes , ' at least for us that was an @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Holy Blossom from its very beginning in 1856 was all about civic engagement and being part of the city of Toronto and contributing to the country , and that 's very much a part of our identity . " <p> Rabbi Splansky sees her role encompassing education as well as advocacy regarding the Jewish community . She encourages members to vote , walking a nonpartisan line while promoting " civic engagement and the responsibility of citizenship , " she says . <p> " I think there 's some people who want synagogues to be only committed to religious fulfilment . But for a synagogue like Holy Blossom Temple , among many others , civic engagement is a mitzvah , and also part of our responsibility as Jewish citizens of this country . " <p> Welcoming , yet vigilant : Meeting members ' needs <p> Soon after the Oct. 7 , 2023 terrorist attacks in Israel and the subsequent war in Gaza , Rabbi Splansky says she learned that " two parallel exercises " exist : There 's " what needs to be done in order for people to be safe @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ people to feel safe . As the rabbi of my congregation , I 'm constantly running on both those tracks . " <p> Revitalized spaces for a community to thrive <p> Those two tracks are evident in what is called the Renewal Project , the renovation and additions to Holy Blossom 's 1938 structure , completed in 2019 , which include a new atrium , green wall , community gathering spaces , and a cafe . Glass panel and steel beam ceilings across the top of the buildings create naturally lit , inviting , open spaces inside . <p> Rededicated in 2018 , the building 's evolution reflects its respective eras , she says . <p> " The original building , the 1938 structure , was built with poured concrete and heavy oak doors , and the lions of Judah are growling above the doors . And that was in the ' 30s when antisemitism was raging in this country , and the leadership of this congregation then was saying to the city -- Bathurst Street was n't @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ saying in a very public way ' We are here , we 're not going anywhere , and we 're going to hold our heads high . ' <p> " The architecture of our old spaces and our new spaces also tell different stories , " she said , mentioning how new doors with " Shalom Aleichem " on them also include upgraded security features . <p> It 's a balance , she says . " We do want to be inviting and welcoming and inclusive and give a message ' everyone 's welcome here . ' And of course , we have to be vigilant . Both things are true . " <p> Holy Blossom Temple in Toronto <p> School , futures , next generations <p> Holy Blossom 's educational programs are a big part of its thriving multigenerational community , and Rabbi Splansky says two major shifts are afoot . <p> Enrolment at Jewish day schools has gone up since Oct. 7 , and Bialik Hebrew Day School , several blocks to the synagogue 's north , is set to rent six classrooms at Holy Blossom for its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the biggest change , come September , is in the name : The synagogue 's Youth Education Centre , which covers part-time education for JK through Grade 12 , is being renamed The Cotler School , after Irwin Cotler , the former federal Minister of Justice and Attorney General and international human rights advocate . <p> Cotler 's name attached to the school will help Holy Blossom launch its next ambitious chapter , says Rabbi Splansky . <p> " Renaming the school now not only elevates , but galvanizes our work ... that we have this launching pad now that speaks to everyone immediately saying Jewish education for youth and teens is critical work . @@139045212 <h> Editorial Consensus on International Criminal Court <p> The International Criminal Court ( ICC ) is seen in The Hague , Netherlands . <p> In the coming months , Caribbean countries will probably find themselves pressured on another front in their relationship with the United States -- over the International Criminal Court ( ICC ) . Jamaica may believe that it has dodged this bullet . While it signed the Rome statute , Kingston has n't ratified the treaty for accession to the court . So , apart from The Bahamas , Jamaica is the only independent member of the Caribbean Community ( CARICOM ) that is n't a party to the ICC.Their seeming status as outsiders may appear to insulate Kingston and Nassau from Washington 's threat to dismantle the court , including potentially applying sanctions against those who resist its efforts . But , as small states , Jamaica and The Bahamas must know that they have more to lose from actions that diminish the rule of law , elevate impunity and , ultimately , undermine democracy . No one is safe in those circumstances.That is why these @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as a political leader in the Caribbean , must n't merely fold in the face of the muscle-flexing by the US secretary of state , Marco Rubio . CARICOM should urgently seek to craft a common regional position on the ICC , in whose creation the region , especially Trinidad and Tobago , played a crucial role . Moreover , the region has loaned its intellectual and legal expertise to international tribunals that dispensed the kind of justice codified in the ICC.With over 120 current members , the ICC , based in The Hague , prosecutes people accused of genocide , crimes against humanity , and war crimes , when national courts are either unwilling or unable to prosecute such crimes ; or if such cases are referred to it by the UN Security Council.The concept that some crimes are so heinous that international justice may be necessary to prevent the perpetrators from escaping punishment is not new . Indeed , it is what the victors , led by the United States , applied to the political leaders and generals of Nazi Germany at the Nuremberg trials after the second @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ prior to the launch of the ICC , eminent Caribbean jurists , with support from regional governments , have played key roles in international criminal tribunals modelled off Nuremberg.For instance , the Kittian judge , Sir Dennis Byron , who later became president of the Caribbean Court of Justice ( CCJ ) , presided over the International Criminal Tribunal on Rwanda that tried perpetrators of that country 's 1990s genocide . Jamaica 's Patrick Robinson , who later became a judge of the International Court of Justice or the World Court , prior to that served on International Criminal Tribunal for the former Yugoslavia , which heard cases of war crimes and genocide out of the Balkans wars of the 1990s . Justice Robinson presided over the war crimes and genocide case of the former Serbian President Slobodan Milosevic , who died in the midst of the trial.The ICC , in its current form , can be traced substantially to the efforts of the former Trinidad and Tobago prime minister , A N R Robinson . It was Mr Robinson , in a 1989 speech at the United Nations , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ prosecute the world 's worst crimes , especially those perpetrated by unaccountable international leaders . Mr Robinson 's work culminated with the launch of the Rome Statute a decade later.While the United States signed the Rome Statute , it , like Jamaica , never ratified the treaty . Washington has consistently made it clear that not only is it not a party to the treaty , its nationals , under no circumstances , would be bound by its statutes , even for crimes committed in other jurisdictions.Jamaica has offered no such reservation . The current Andrew Holness administration has been quiet on the court . @@139097949 <p> While recently decluttering , I came across two plastic totes filled with diaries , clippings , scrapbooks , cards and other papers . Amongst several dozen diaries were two books that I knew must be saved . <p> From Feb. 15 , 1996 , until Dec. 26 , 2004 , about three times per week , I wrote about my first child , my daughter Savannah , who was born on July 29 , 1993 . I can not remember what prompted me to begin what I dubbed " Savannah 's Life . " I suspect it had to do with the many gaps in my own turbulent childhood and the desire to put down on paper what my firstborn was doing , knowing I would never remember such minutiae decades later . I figured that recording my daughter 's life would be a gift to her . <p> In the Savannah diaries , the left page was reserved for photos , birthday party invitations , cards , little paper items she made , library reading lists , sports milestones , school items and other visual artifacts . The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ time has , in the decades since , evolved into a map of childhood before and after the arrival of the moment when digital technology began to rapidly change our lives . <p> The two books chronicle the life of a middle-class child living on Vancouver Island . It 's also a time capsule of sorts , documenting the rise of Sailor Moon , Polly Pocket and Spice Girls and the evolution of Halloween costumes and elementary school education . Notable was the large amount of time spent playing outside , weekly library visits and regular sleepovers . <p> Reading them now , after 21 years , is fascinating . Did I remember that Savannah played with imaginary friends and , according to her , " It 's my business , " what they say ? Or that when she was in my belly , she told me , she sucked her thumb and ate mashed potatoes ? Or how our Japanese exchange student loved her blue eyes and blonde hair ? Or her sleep-talking , like the time she said , " I do n't want to eat squash @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ bike on Father 's Day ? Or her note to the tooth fairy saying she was worried she had n't lost any teeth recently and to put a spell on mom because her tooth was sore ? Layered in was the documentation of childhood sicknesses and day care trials , her Barney the dinosaur infatuation and the many birthday party invitations . <p> While my aim was to diarize the mundane through to the marvellous , the uniting thread was truth , albeit through my eyes . I do recall , from university lectures , that the interpretation of history depends on the writer , so what I wrote was coloured by the perspective of a working , harried mother . But I also have a journalism degree , so I like to think that recording facts was a priority . Without an accurate record , our past years become susceptible to the personal edits driven by exaggeration , wishful thinking or outright fabrication . How easy it is to rewrite our stories when there are no primary documents to consult . <p> Are diaries still relevant ? I no @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of anyone who does , although it is a private endeavour and perhaps the practice continues at a smaller scale . If I need to verify or stoke a memory , I can usually resurrect it by looking at my hundreds of date-stamped digital photos or archived e-mails . But they are incomplete and intangible . <p> In the Savannah diaries , the left page was used for visual artifacts such as photos and birthday party invitations.Courtesy of Savannah Moneo . <p> These days , we can easily record moments in our lives , just as we can easily edit them to burnish reality . We can type words about our feelings and even if they are not accurate , consider them so . Is there a line between fact and fanciful thinking , or does it matter ? <p> In the early days of social media , sites like Facebook and Instagram and Twitter ( now X ) became de facto diaries , where digital entries documented all manner of personal events and thoughts . Interestingly , there are websites , such as Twitario , that allow users to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ users of social media sites are also often " digital creators , " perhaps imbuing them with the urge to sprinkle fantasy into their creations . <p> Capturing an honest , full picture of our past days is becoming another casualty of technology . Carefully arranged photo albums , ticket stubs from concerts and plays , much-loved recipes are all physical items being discarded in favour of sleek , paper-free lives . For many of us , the photos and recipes reside on our devices . No wonder losing your phone is a distinctly 21st-century crisis . <p> Beyond lamenting the disappearance of the locked diaries of teenage girls and the innocence represented in these pink books , there is the dwindling of sober reflection of our daily lives . Sitting with your thoughts , mentally composing them and putting them down on a paper requires concentration , not fast fingers . Reflection is also required when writing in a journal , which is a close relative to a diary . But the two are like a police officer investigating a crime scene versus a screenwriter setting a scene . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ can be emotion-based and veer into pep talks . <p> In existence for centuries , diaries had a business-related genesis , serving as record-keeping for Mesopotamian merchants , eventually evolving to travel journals , documentation of military travails and religious confessionals . But it was in the late Renaissance that diaries took flight . One of the most famous was Samuel Pepys ' , whose diary from January , 1660 , to May , 1669 , captured the chaos of London . <p> As mass literacy was achieved and paper became cheaper , diaries flourished , but by the mid-1800s the phrase " Dear Diary " took on a melodramatic cast . Not so for Anne Frank , who began writing to " Kitty " on her 13th birthday , June 12 , 1942 . She continued documenting her isolated life under Nazi terror until August , 1944 . One wonders if somewhere in Gaza or Sudan , a young girl has recorded recent events through a similar lens of reality and produced grains of truth . <p> Finding fertile environments for these grains of truth is a challenge today @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ brain to hand to paper , a physical passage that will withstand power outages and cyber attacks . <p> Savannah 's two diaries are precious and now belong to her . My hope is that by having them , if she later has a child , it will fuel a desire in her to put down her phone , pick up a pen , open a blank book and start a diary of her own .

But relying on future roundtables to address students ' grievances , while reducing class time so the course can be digitally documented , comes as a disappointment . <p> I do not fault Clinton for these issues . Perhaps the most enthralling moments were thanks to the many guests who shared the stage with Clinton during the semester , including Peter Clement , a former deputy director of the CIA , and David Miliband , a former foreign secretary of the U.K. Parliament . <p> The author ( right ) posing a question to Clinton ( onstage , left ) , Maria Ressa ( onstage , center ) and Yarhi-Milo ( onstage , right ) at Columbia University in 2023 . <p> Courtesy of Cate Twining-Ward <p> Advertisement <p> The day I fought for the mic and secured it , I was able to ask my question to Clinton , Yarhi-Milo and Nobel Peace Prize winner and journalist @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ hold different citizenships and grew up around the world , I explained that I 've experienced rejection based solely on my nationality when working as a journalist in other countries . <p> I noted that I understood and in many cases agreed with the sentiment behind this rejection , given that the media is often saturated with Western narratives and journalists who are telling stories about places they are not from and may not know well . However , I continued , in places where there is extreme censorship and citizens can not tell their own stories , how can journalists ethically tell stories on their behalf ? <p> Clinton nodded reassuringly as I spoke , and Ressa smiled . She then said that whoever declined my work on the basis of citizenship was not worth my time . Integrity of facts is crucial , Ressa said , adding that as long as " you 're honest , " every journalist is unique and " that alone makes you qualified to tell stories . " <p> Advertisement <p> Clinton 's own unique stories were perhaps the most valuable part of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ during the killing of Osama bin Laden , and her accounts of the time she spent with Russian President Vladimir Putin in his bunker , are anecdotes I could n't get from any other professor teaching at SIPA . <p> Despite this , Clinton 's course has provoked many students in a time of heightened tensions . It has also made me question Columbia 's institutional priorities and its ability to effectively address sensitive issues with integrity -- trade-offs that seem to benefit the university 's image at the expense of its students . It 's not right for the school to commodify their students , turning them into audience members and then often receding to the ivory tower when the conversation gets uncomfortable . <p> I am also discouraged that neither Clinton nor the dean attended a single weekly discussion section , let alone read the assignments we poured hours of work into writing . Instead , these duties were handed off to the teachers assigned to lead the discussion groups . Similarly , office hours , a common resource provided by professors for the benefit of students , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ students , and it could have been easily implemented , whether on zoom or in person . Regardless of their titles , backgrounds or schedules , professors should be held accountable for cutting corners . <p> Advertisement <p> Just before our final lecture of the semester , I asked several of my classmates if they would recommend the course to other students . Their responses were mixed . One particularly positive student said : " It 's not about what I learned , it 's the fact that I can put a class taught by Hillary Clinton on my resume ... That 's the value . But I could have learned everything just from reading her memoir . " <p> Our last class , held on Wednesday , was supposed to be dedicated to answering student questions . However , two days before the class , we were told that only 20 minutes would be allocated to live questions . The rest of the time , we learned , would feature Clinton and Yarhi-Milo reading aloud selected questions that we were instructed to email ahead of time . Of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ policy issues -- Hillary Clinton 's final question to the dean was : " What 's your favorite Taylor Swift song and why ? " <p> The course has tremendous potential . But if the university chooses to offer it again in person , there is a lot of work to be done to ensure students experience the scholastic rigor they expect , instead of what was , for all intents and purposes , academic theater . <p> Advertisement <p> Note : A representative for Clinton did not immediately return HuffPost 's request for comment . Courses of this size ( at Columbia and elsewhere ) are always a frontal lecture format , which does not include Q&amp;A from the students . In this case , the professors chose to modify the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ In courses of this size , office hours are typically provided by section leaders . In this case there were 15 sections led by Ph.D . experts in the field . Having section leaders hold smaller discussion sections and grade papers is a universal operating procedure for lectures of this size . This is done so that the maximum number of students can engage in material presented by a professor , while ensuring that students receive hands-on instruction and consistency in grading over the duration of the course ... <p> Both Secretary Clinton and Dean Yarhi-Milo have been adamant about the need for difficult conversations that challenge individual assumptions , and this is part of what they modeled in class ... <p> In addition to answering student questions live each class , both have engaged in programming outside of class on current issues of global policy , including the war in Gaza . <p> The author told HuffPost she stands by her essay . <p> Cate Twining-Ward is a 2023 SIPA Environmental Fellow at Columbia University and studies environmental science and policy at the Columbia Climate School and School of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ United Nations and as a senior correspondent for Planet Forward , an environmental journalism organization . <p> Do you have a compelling personal story you 'd like to see published on HuffPost ? @@139119532 <p> Yemen 's Iran-aligned Houthis will impose a maritime blockade on Saudi Arabia , they said on Monday , further throttling a global energy market already greatly restricted by Iran 's closure of the Strait of Hormuz . <p> This is why it matters and what it means for the Iran war and the global energy crisis : <h> How big is the risk to global energy markets ? <p> It is not clear how the Houthis would carry out a maritime blockade of Saudi Arabia , its northern neighbour along the Red Sea coast , or whether it would include a return to attacks on shipping . <p> Yemen sits on the Bab el-Mandeb strait -- the southern gateway to the Red Sea -- and closing that would open up a new front in the energy crisis and Iran 's overarching conflict with the U.S. <p> With Hormuz already disrupted , the Red Sea has become a critical alternative outlet for Gulf oil and other products . A serious disruption would mean both of the Middle East 's major oil export routes are shut simultaneously . <p> Iran 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the U.S. attacked it on Feb. 28 disrupted most oil and other exports from the Gulf , raising prices and delivering a global energy shock . <p> Saudi Arabia responded by diverting more than 70 per cent of its normal daily crude exports to the Red Sea port of Yanbu . Ships from Yanbu bound for Europe go north through the Suez Canal . Those heading to Asia go south through Bab el-Mandeb . <p> Shipments from Yanbu averaged 4 million barrels per day in recent weeks according to data from Kpler and Signal Ocean , up from around 973,000 bpd a year earlier . <p> Total petroleum volumes transiting Bab el-Mandeb amounted to 7.4 million bpd in June , or about 7 per cent of global oil output , according to Kpler data , up from 4.2 million bpd last year . <p> That has provided a lifeline for the energy market , helping to keep down global oil prices . Saudi Arabia is considering an expansion of its crude oil pipeline to the Red Sea coast , Reuters reported last week . <p> When the Houthis launched attacks @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ exports were flowing freely . <p> Houthi supporters rally against the Saudi-led coalition 's restrictions on Houthi-controlled areas , which the group describes as a blockade , in Sanaa , Yemen , July 17 . Khaled Abdullah/Reuters <h> Who are the Houthis and are they closing energy routes on behalf of Iran ? <p> The Houthis emerged as a military , political and religious movement in north Yemen in the 1990s , fighting guerrilla wars against the government in Sanaa . <p> They have been in a civil war against the Saudi-backed , internationally recognized , government for more than a decade and have attacked Gulf neighbours with missiles and drones . <p> However , a 2022 truce between the country 's warring sides largely held until last week , when Yemen 's internationally recognized government said it had struck Sanaa airport to stop an Iranian plane landing . <p> The Houthis said Saudi Arabia was responsible and in response fired missiles at Abha airport in the kingdom 's mountainous southwest . <p> A senior Houthi official , politburo member Mohammad al-Farah , then warned in an interview on Iran @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , Bab el-Mandeb would be closed . <p> Iran champions the Houthis as part of its regional " Axis of Resistance , " which includes Lebanon 's Hezbollah and Iraqi Shi'ite militias , though its ties with the Yemeni movement are less clear than with those other groups . <p> The Houthis do not recognize Iran 's supreme leader as their ultimate religious authority in the same way Hezbollah and the Iraqi groups do . Their motivations are mainly domestic , though the group is ideologically aligned with Iran . <p> The U.S. says Iran has armed , funded and trained the Houthis with help from Hezbollah . The Houthis deny being an Iranian proxy and say they develop their own weapons . It is not clear how far the group 's stance on Bab el-Mandeb and the Red Sea stems from its own strategic priorities or is being made on Iran 's behalf . <p> Houthi supporters sit around an Iranian flag , as they rally against the Saudi-led coalition 's restrictions on Houthi-controlled areas , in Sanaa , Yemen , July 17 . @@139145475 <p> A comment from a reader from the June 2026 excerpt of Phoebe Maltz Bovy 's book excerpt from The Walrus . <p> What oh what is going on at PEN America ? It is a time-honoured case of , there 's no pleasing everybody . And I must alas count myself among the displeased . <p> First , on July 9 , the esteemed free-expression organization ran a report by Lisa Tolin , Geraldine Baum , and Malka Margolies . " ' A Silent Moratorium ' : Interviews with Israeli and Jewish writers who report facing rising isolation and exclusion , " is what it sounds like : a reported article about the shunning of ' Zionists ' in the literary world . Many of the stories themselves will be familiar to readers of this column : the Guernica debacleof 2024 , the Zionist-author-hunting spreadsheet of the same year . Indeed , one writer 's April appearance on The Jewish Angle is even cited : <p> " The author Meg Keene received multiple offers from agents on her romance novel with Jewish themes . One agent told her that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ all Jewish references from the book . " The best friend character was named Yael , and she said we can not sell a book with a character named Yael , we need to rename her , like Sue . And I did n't do those things , and the book did n't sell , " she told Canadian Jewish News . " <p> It is a neither-here-nor-there document . On the one hand , it 's sort of amazing that this thing that 's been on the Jewish community 's radar for years is being taken seriously , complete with concrete examples of things beyond bad vibes that Jewish and Israeli authors have experienced . ( For example : " When children 's author K. Marcus posted a Facebook ad for her new picture book , Frankenstein 's Matzah : A Passover Parody , it was targeted with comments accusing Jews of supporting genocide . Marcus is American and the book is about a child who brings passover matzah to life . " ) <p> On the other , the report brings in , as context , the war @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ only natural that authors in no way personally implicated in that war -- a group of largely left-wing , pro-peace sorts -- would be collateral . It was maybe a step towards something , but was also one of those anti-antisemitism statements that has an ' and Islamophobia is also wrong ' tacked on , a problem not because Islamophobia is grand , but because you are actually allowed to name one issue at a time , and naming a second , unrelated one has a way of falsely presenting things as zero-sum that are not . <p> Then came Dinaw Mengestu , a novelist , deciding that this report was one Zionism too many as far as he was concerned . He promptly resigned as president of the organization . It 's well and good to talk of the free expression rights of Jews , but what about the free expression rights of those who wish to express that they want to hear nothing from Jews , other than self-flagellation ? Do n't they deserve consideration ? ( BDS stuff , effectively . ) <p> Then came a new @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to Our Community , " by co-CEOs Summer Lopez and Clarisse Rosaz Shariyf , that includes a vow " to be better . " They stand by the earlier report , but in a way that involves the holding of a proverbial nose with one hand and the other , outstretched , holding something revolting , head turned away . They explicate at length about exactly how awful Israel is , how nobly anti-Israel PEN has been . Whereas the July 9 report spoke of " what human rights organizations and experts have determined to be genocide in Gaza , " the July 15 statement takes a more direct line : " We have said plainly , and continue to believe , that what has happened in Gaza is a genocide . " Who exactly " we " are gets complicated here , all the more so given that this is a free expression group . <p> The missing piece is , as always , the mechanism by which boycotting Jewish authors who failed to pose for their headshot photos in keffiyehs is helpful to Palestinians . If the position @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ blame so readily sorted out , that any writer , anywhere , who does not have that issue front-and-centre and have a certain take on it is in the wrong , then at least there 'd be consistency . I would n't be on board , and it would still amount to the suppression of ideas and therefore free expression , but it would be , as I said , consistent . <p> The discrimination is at the level of acceptance of collateral . Of saying that yes , authors are being shunned on the basis of identity traits and not even political stances . There 's this casual decision that because it feels somehow pro-Palestinian to turn Jews ( back ) into pariahs , to be straight-up antisemitic , the feelings of those who feel this trump ( word choice intentional ) the realities . This from a ' literary community ' so sure it holds moral high grounds , and from an organization therein that wants to present itself as on the side of the good . It 's pretty gross , is the thing . But in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that . At least PEN handed the scrap it did . Or something . <p> So that 's the overview of the PEN thing . But there 's a part I want to hone in on , because it 's important as well as easily missed . <p> The initial PEN report presents the plight of Jewish and Israeli authors as a crisis for literary " diversity , " which is , I think , only part of the picture . It highlights the efforts from within the Jewish community to offer what the mainstream publishing industry is not : <p> I do not wish to poo-poo the efforts themselves , which are worthy , at least in motivation . But what we 're looking at is a siloing , wherein there is a place for Jewish books , but it 's at a Jewish Books table . That 's already a problem if the hope is that the Jewish book has some non-Jewish readers as well , although this points to a wider issue in 2010s-and-onwards book publishing , with everything needing to be categorizable along empowering-or-tragic identity lines @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for very different reasons than that of other identities . The Jewish Books table is there not to remedy past discrimination , but the ongoing sort . <p> But a Jewish-authored book that is not about Jewish themes , that does not belong on a Jewish Books table , is whatever the literary equivalent is of politically homeless . <p> This is not a new topic for me in my capacity as writer at The CJN ( see this 2024 column ) but it has if anything gotten more extreme . Whereas Jewish authors once fought to be recognized as authors who happened to be Jewish , Jewish authors now get classified as anti-Zionist or not-anti-Zionist ( aka , for publishing-industry purposes , mega-Zionist ) , or as whichever is the bad one to be in someone else 's view at any given time , as is the situation for Etgar Keret , who " describes himself as having ' this unique status of being boycotted both in Israel and overseas . ' " <p> If you 're Jewish but you want to write about something not topic-taggable as Jewish @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the work itself , and in your author bio , and also go back in time and have never , in your capacities as a private citizen or in your day job or wherever else , have ever said anything about anything Jewish-related . <p> Given that my workplace is literally a place called The Canadian Jewish News , I am a rather openly Jewish author . I 've also been an editor at the Forward and been profiled in Tablet . My doctorate is in French , but about French Jews . <p> But I am a woman of many interests . I am also the author of a not particularly Jewish new book , The Last Straight Woman : On Desiring Men . I got the book deal in July 2023 , during what was in retrospect this microscopic window between the 2010s-early-2020s hyperwoke era in publishing where anything even suspected of heterodoxy was off-limits , and the post-Oct. 7 one where every Jewish author was assessed according to Zionist-or-anti . <p> I gave my own Jewishness very little thought when it came to a side project for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and non-expert thoughts about the Middle East . It would never in a million years have occurred to me , in July 2023 , to view my hopes for a two-state solution as at all related to a book about straight women and decades-old Britcoms . <p> I was , however , plenty conscious of my status as a member of what the author Meghan Daum calls " problematized . " Not cancelled , but someone who expressed less-than-party-line views during the 2010s . I had written a 2017 book criticizing privilege-checking , and was one of the signatories of the 2020 Harper 's letter . <p> Then there 's the book 's subject matter . Even though The Last Straight Woman is a feminist book , the mere fact of a straight woman writing about heterosexuality was a bit hmm for a moment where book publishing was all about a focus on books that could be sold as overtly promoting of social justice aims . The obstacles I anticipated and encountered , initially , were about perceived problematicness , either my own or that of the project itself . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ excerpt in June , and a number of the replies ( I saved screenshots , then did a spot of blocking ) were about the apparent love letter I had written to Benjamin Netanyahu . " Are we calling genocide supporter ' cultural critics now ' " was one . " Of course she 's a Zionist too , " another . Another called me a " gender-traitor zionist , " which I 'm sure makes sense in this person or bot 's mind . <p> I realize this does not make me seem very quick on the uptake , but here goes : It was only then that it occurred to me that I was not merely an author of a straight-women book whose day job included writing and conducting interviews about the silencing of Jewish authors . I was also one of those authors . My book is Jewish enough ( by virtue of its author ) to be shunned by anyone boycotting ' Zionists , ' but not enough so that if I did n't happen to work for The CJN , The CJN would have much @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be ! Community publications and Judaica bookstores and bookstore sections have existed for eons , as is correct . What 's new ( or newly-returned ) is their role as the only possible place for works that are only incidentally Jewish . <p> I started thinking about various post-Oct. 7 publishing-world setbacks I had experienced in a whole new light . Without going into specifics , certain things that had made no sense suddenly did . The room was read , and I did n't belong in it . Not because of specifics about me as a thinker -- the vibe had shifted ; my not being hyperwoke was no longer an issue , and I 'm if anything a sappy Resistance Lib by 2026 standards . Nope , it 's the Jewish thing . <p> Of course , if the vibe is antisemitic , you can not debate a vibe . You can not point out contradictions or hypocrisies in a vibe . Again , the first PEN report : <p> " It is difficult to assess how much of what the writers PEN America spoke to are experiencing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ war ; how much from anti-Israel , anti-Zionist , or antisemitic sentiment ; and how much reflects matters of business or taste , which are also shaped by geopolitics . " <p> " PEN says that it is difficult to assess how much of what writers are experiencing " reflects matters of business or taste . " It is hard to imagine PEN would explain away the exclusion of another disfavored minority 's books as a question of ' business or taste . ' " <p> This has been the question in publishing for some time , both in Wokeness 1.0 , with its myriad and ever-evolving identitarian preoccupations , and Wokeness 2.0 , the Palestine-is-everything omnicause . It 's not always clear where the line falls between publishing industry gatekeepers earnestly adhering to progressive politics , and workers in that industry making calculated sales-and-branding decisions . As in , is it that the acquisitions editor 's heart bleeds for the dead children of Gaza ( but they napped through Oct. 7 itself , possibly ) ? Or are they just a professional reader-of-rooms , and they 've assessed that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the case , the effect is a siloing , a re-ghettoization , and the end of all manner of hybrid literary , comedic , and artistic forms as we knew them . <h> Author <p> Phoebe is the opinion editor for The Canadian Jewish News and a contributor editor of The CJN 's Scribe Quarterly print magazine . " <p> There 's a lot of that going around . <p> It is impossible , especially in this climate , to hold a sports tournament in a vacuum , completely divorced from the politics of the day . @@139207037 <p> B'nai Brith Canada often hears about " awful but lawful " behaviour that does n't get included in Statistics Canada 's hate crimes report . ( Photo provided by B'nai Brith Canada ) <p> Jews continue to be the most targeted religious group in Canada even as the number of religion-motivated police-reported hate crimes fell in 2025 , according to a new Statistics Canada report . <p> Around 4,700 hate crimes were reported to police in 2025 , with 788 targeting Jewish community members . This is an 18 per cent decrease from the 943 recorded incidents involving Jews reported in 2024 . There were also 204 reported hate crimes against Muslim Canadians for a decrease of 11 per cent , and 36 hate crimes against Catholics , representing a 39 per cent decrease . <p> Black Canadians were the most targeted group among all categories in 2025 at 858 reported incidents . South Asian Canadians were the third most targeted and saw their sharpest year-to-year increase since 2020 -- with 483 incidents , an increase of 49 per cent in 2025 from 2024 . <p> Still , Canadian @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of all religion-motivated hate crimes in 2025 . It 's a proportion that Mark Sandler , chair of the Alliance of Canadians Combatting Antisemitism ( ALCCA ) , has seen from hate crime Statistics Canada reports as far back as 2022 . <p> " The numbers have varied from 69 per cent to 71 per cent over the last couple of years , so it 's no surprise to me , " he says . " We do see , when there 's conflict in the Middle East , an uptick in antisemitic hate crimes , but this has exploded exponentially . " <p> Sandler says there are many potential reasons Jews continue to be targeted , from the " demonization of those who support Israel and its right to exist " to social media algorithms that allow antisemitism to spread . <p> " You have antisemitism coming at you from at least three different fronts : from the left , from the far right , and from Islamist radicals , " Sandler says . <p> Noah Shack , CEO of the Centre for Israel and Jewish Affairs ( CIJA @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reinforces the seriousness of Canada 's antisemitism crisis . <p> " To put these figures in perspective , a Jewish Canadian is 22 times more likely to be the victim of a hate crime than any other Canadian , " Shack wrote . " Much of today 's antisemitic violence -- which includes gunfire and firebombing of synagogues and schools , vandalizing businesses , and assaulting community members -- is perpetrated by extremists at home and foreign actors abroad , driven by conspiracy theories and anti-Israel hate . " <p> Shack says the government and authorities need to recognize modern forms of antisemitism , enforce the law more consistently when it comes to Jew-hatred , and make sure public institutions are n't " weaponized against Canadians . " <p> " Governments and authorities must be unequivocal : nothing can justify these criminal acts targeting Canadians , " he says . <p> Warren Silver is a national training officer for the policing services program at Statistics Canada . He 's been involved in hate crime data collection since 2002 . <p> Silver says he ca n't explain why hate crimes targeting @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ tracking data rather than doing a qualitative review . But he says many factors , including increases in the public 's awareness of antisemitism or collaborations between police and Jewish communities , " can influence the numbers greatly . " <p> " Almost every police service that I 'm aware of has been working , ( over ) the last few years , with community outreach , " Silver says , adding that this includes connecting with Jewish organizations like B'nai Brith Canada and Friends of Simon Wiesenthal Center ( FSWC ) . <p> Jaime Kirzner-Roberts , senior director of policy and advocacy for FSWC , says she 's seen police forces expand their hate crime divisions over the last few years . " FSWC also receives reglar calls from community members who say police dismissed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> " There is a lack of uniformity in approach and best practices around hate crime , " Kirzner-Roberts says . <p> She says the decrease in the number of hate crimes targeting Jews between 2024 and 2025 is " relatively minor " so she 's " not sure there are major forces behind it . " There 's quite a bit of underreporting of hate crimes . " <p> Silver agrees that hate crimes are underreported . One of these surveys in 2019 found that only about 29 per cent @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of research and advocacy for B'nai Brith Canada , says 2025 's decrease in incidents is part of a " general ebb and flow . " He adds that the Statistics Canada report does n't capture " awful but lawful " behaviour that falls under the strict threshold of a hate crime , which B'nai Brith Canada hears about on a regular basis . <p> Robertson says some Jewish community members might not want to report hate crimes because they might have been ignored by police in the past . <p> " This is a PR problem that law enforcement needs to address , " he says . @@139242197 <p> Nearly every week , I see newspapers and magazines that seemed fine suddenly going out of business . Or a private equity fund buying up a chain of newspapers that had been serving communities for decades and stripping them for parts . And now , the thing that 's making all publishers wring their hands is AI -- publishers are seeing massive drops to their web visits as AI scrapes their content and delivers it up to people in chatbots , with the journalists who did the original work not even credited with the work . <p> Despite this , I have hope for journalism because of what 's happening at The Tyee . <p> Years ago , we made a strategic bet that support from our readers was going to drive our whole future . And thankfully , that is working for us . <p> Over the past decade , we 've more than doubled our journalism team and are publishing more work than ever . More people read The Tyee now than at any point in our 23-year history . <p> This is only possible because we regularly ask our readers to sign up as paying Tyee Builder members . Roughly half of our entire budget is made up of contributions from over 10,000 readers . <p> We 've been doing more as our resources have grown . A weekend culture section filled with original essays . Hard-hitting investigative journalism in Alberta . A brand new biodiversity beat . A reporting project called Reality Check , focusing on misinformation and online radicalization . A flagship podcast . <p> Our model is simple : if more of our readers sign up to support The Tyee , we can keep up with rising costs and keep adding more talented journalists to our roster and set them loose on uncovering truths that would otherwise not see the light of day . <p> Nearly every week , I see newspapers and magazines that seemed fine suddenly going out of business . E-mail addresses are only used for the purposes of Tyee-related correspondence or comment moderation . <h> Stop Unpaid Work for WestJet Flight Attendants <p> Imagine showing up to your job , putting on your uniform , doing mandatory safety checks and assisting customers ... all before you start getting paid . <p> For WestJet flight attendants , it 's standard practice . WestJet flight attendants are n't paid until the aircraft doors close and the brakes are off . This means all the critical work they do during boarding , deplaning and ground delays goes entirely uncompensated . On average , that adds up to 35 hours of unpaid labour every single month . <p> In an economy where every dollar counts , it is fundamentally unjust to demand free labour from frontline safety professionals . <h> Tyee Commenting Guidelines <p> Please note that email notifications for replies are not currently working due to a software issue which may be resolved in a future update . <p> Comments that violate guidelines risk being deleted , and violations may result in a temporary or permanent user ban . We have definitely crossed that boundary with regard to the wars currently being fought up and down the strip of land between the longitudes of 40 ? E and 50 ? E ( Eastern Ukraine/Persian Gulf/Israel , Gaza and Lebanon ) . <h> or <p> I have been writing this column twice a week for a long time , so I have a finely developed sense for when the audience is sick of a subject . We have definitely crossed that boundary with regard to the wars currently being fought up and down the strip of land between the longitudes of 40 ? E @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Lebanon ) . <p> It 's not just that they are stupid wars , launched by men who were too ignorant or too arrogant to realize that they could not win them ( Vladimir Putin , Donald Trump , Benjamin Netanyahu ) , and it 's certainly not because they are harmless wars . <p> Not only are they killing thousands of people every week , but in every case the country that started the war also has nuclear weapons . In each case there is a small but real possibility that the leader in question might resort to using at least one " tactical " nuclear weapon as a final warning rather than accept the blame for a humiliating failure to achieve his aims . <p> Yet nothing much new and interesting is happening from one day to the next in any of these wars , and the audience would really like to move on . I have been trying to write about something else at least once a week , so I thought : maybe I could do a piece about Kim Jong Un 's teenage daughter @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Just fluff , really , but it 's better than producing nothing to fill that space . <p> So I go online to update my limited knowledge of North Korean politics . Surprise ! The last time most news outlets paid much attention to North Korea was in 2017-18 , when Donald Trump decided that he would save the world from the threat of North Korean nuclear weapons . <p> As they used to say about the Roman empire : All Roads Lead to Donald Trump . But it was quite instructive , because it shows just how limited Trump 's repertoire of negotiating tactics really is . What can his first-term attempt to stop North Korea from getting nuclear weapons tell us about the likely outcome of his current attempt to bring Iran to heel ? Everything . <p> When he took power in early 2017 , Trump seemed obsessed by the " threat " of North Korean nuclear weapons . He made it the No. 1 issue of his foreign policy agenda . He said that North Korean nuclear weapons " will be met with fire and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ promised to " totally destroy the country , " and called Kim Jong Un " Little Rocket Man . " <p> It was all the invective we 're now hearing again , almost word for word , but then came the big switch . <p> " I trust him , " Trump burbled when the two men finally met in Singapore in 2018 . " He has a great personality . We fell in love through letters . " <p> They met once more on the line dividing North Korea from South Korea ( the " DMZ " ) in 2019 , and that was it . <p> No treaty , no further discussions , nothing . Within a year , Trump had totally lost interest . Meanwhile North Korea 's arsenal of nuclear weapons has grown to 60 warheads , according to a new estimate by the Stockholm International Peace Research Institute , and Pyongyang has recently tested an ICBM that can reach anywhere in the United States . <p> Will Trump pull the same vanishing act with Iran ? Well , he already has with regard to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ way down the road in the " Memorandum of Understanding " he signed last month . That does n't matter much , because Iran never had any nukes and was n't even trying very hard to get them . <p> The current round of fighting is just about who controls the Strait of Hormuz , a huge and previously unexploited strategic asset that Trump 's attack brought to the attention of the Iranian regime . With the price of oil going back up and the mid-term election looming near , Trump is quite likely to chicken out again . <p> So now we can get back to the real question : what will become of sweet little Kim Ju Ae , the 13-year-old daughter of North Korean dictator Kim Jong Un ?
