@@139314875 <p> Alia Offman shook and sobbed as her mind transported her to a Nazi gas chamber . She slowed her breathing , feeling as if she was reliving the sensory details of a memory . <p> Only , this memory was not hers . <p> It happened during what psychedelic practitioners such as herself call " integration " -- the days , weeks or months after a psychedelic experience , when people process , interpret and make meaning of altered states of consciousness . <p> A registered psychologist in Ontario , Offman had spent nearly two years training in psychedelic therapy , consuming different psychedelic substances and immersing herself in meditation . <p> One afternoon in June 2024 , after participating in an aromatherapy circle , breathing in the scents of rose geranium essential oil , she was jolted into the experience of being " somewhere else , at another time . " <p> In her mind , she found herself standing in the cold , concrete confines of a gas chamber , watching four children die . " They were not my children , " she said . She @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ knew relatively little about her family 's experience in the Holocaust . She was aware that her great-aunt Miriam had been murdered , but little more . It was n't until months after that visualization , after speaking with relatives and reviewing family records , that she learned more about what happened . <p> Her great-aunt Miriam had remained in Poland with four children when Offman 's grandfather , Miriam 's brother , immigrated to Canada in 1928 . The family that stayed in Europe were murdered at Treblinka . <p> Only later did another detail stop Offman cold : when she experienced the vision , her own four children were the same ages Miriam 's children had been when they died . Is it possible , she wondered , that her journey with psychedelics unlocked some sort of ancestral memory ? <p> The question is not isolated . As psychedelics have gained renewed attention as tools for healing and spiritual exploration , therapists and researchers have documented reports of participants describing experiences they interpret as ancestral or intergenerational . Emerging research suggests psychedelic states can bring forward emotionally charged @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ family history or inherited trauma . Scientists caution that these experiences should not automatically be read as historical truth , but as symbolic expressions of psychological content that may otherwise remain inaccessible . <p> Whether such experiences represent ancestral memories or symbolic narratives remains an open question . But many clinicians say the meaning people draw from these images can itself become an important part of healing . <p> For Offman , the experience became a turning point . Over the next two years , she slowly worked to unpack it through meditation , Jewish study and what she calls psychedelic cannabis -- the intentional therapeutic use of cannabis ( a drug not otherwise considered psychedelic ) in carefully structured sessions to access altered states of consciousness . <p> " It all occurred post-Oct. 7 , " she said . " I think that was a huge trigger for it all because it 's really brought up that level of maternal insecurity that emotionally ties me to my own family , but also to a whole long history of the grief of Jewish mothers . " <p> That work , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ itself , reshaping how she thinks about psychedelic therapy for her Jewish patients . <p> It also guided her towards a conversation now emerging in Jewish communities across the world : whether psychedelics can become not only a tool for treating trauma , but also a pathway back into Jewish ritual , mysticism and identity . <p> The therapy room <p> Offman , who lives near Ottawa , facilitates psychedelic cannabis sessions in her therapeutic practice . Cannabis is legal in Canada ; psilocybin , LSD and ayahuasca -- substances more commonly classified as psychedelics -- remain illegal outside specific authorized contexts . She uses cannabis not as a casual intoxicant but as part of a structured therapeutic process . <p> " I train people in a mindfulness-based practice to begin , and this is because I want my clients to be prepared , " Offman explained . <p> That preparation usually takes four to six weeks . Clients learn mindfulness practices , such as breath work and mantras , so they can remain regulated during the session . Then they come to a dedicated space in Offman 's basement @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ designed for comfort and intention . <p> She describes it as large , warm and lived-in , with light colours : blues , beiges and whites . There is a couch , a blanket , eye shades and carefully chosen music . Clients begin with guided meditation , consume cannabis , lie down and turn inward . <p> " One of the misunderstandings around psychedelic sessions is that people think that the psychedelics are introduced and then they 're talking while under the influence , " she said . " But it 's more of a 90-minute , eyes-closed session . " <p> The music , she said , " carries the emotional tone , " helping guide clients through waves of feeling without overwhelming them . Afterward , clients usually spend about a week integrating the experience before returning to unpack it in therapy . <p> " When you 're signing up to do psychedelics with me , you 're signing up for a whole course of therapy , " she said . " It 's not just the psychedelic session . " <p> That distinction is central to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . The substance may open a door , but it does not do the healing on its own . <p> For Offman , Jewish psychedelic care became urgent after Oct. 7 . She said Jewish trauma is often invisible in therapeutic settings , especially when non-Jewish therapists view Jewish patients simply as " white . " She explained that rising antisemitism , Holocaust distortion and intergenerational fear add to the problem . <p> " We need to have culturally competent care for Jews when it comes to therapeutic settings , " she said , adding that the use of certain substances can be one piece of a larger puzzle . <p> She also sees a spiritual opening . Long drawn to yoga , meditation , Eastern philosophy and neuroscience , Offman wondered why so many Jews in psychedelic and mindfulness spaces seemed to leave Jewish language behind . <p> It is a pattern with deep roots . Some of the most influential Jewish figures in the modern psychedelic story turned toward Eastern spiritual systems to explain the states they encountered . Richard Alpert , the Harvard psychologist who worked with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on Hinduism and Buddhism . Rick Strassman , the psychiatrist known for his research on DMT , a psychedelic , later wrote about the Hebrew Bible and prophecy after years of engagement with Buddhist practice . Rick Doblin , the founder of the Multidisciplinary Association for Psychedelic Studies , has spoken about Jewish suffering and the Holocaust as part of the moral background to his work . Michael Pollan , whose 2018 book How to Change Your Mind helped bring psychedelics into mainstream conversation , is Jewish , though his writing largely frames the psychedelic revival through science , consciousness and spirituality rather than specifically Jewish practice . <p> For Offman , the question became why Jews needed to borrow so much language from elsewhere when Jewish mysticism already contained a rich vocabulary for altered states , oneness and the soul . <p> These thoughts led her to studying Kabbalah , the body of Jewish mystical tradition that explores the nature of God , creation , the soul and hidden spiritual structures of reality . <p> " As soon as I read it , it made complete sense to me @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ this beautiful roadmap for understanding how we take this kind of esoteric religious or cultural experience and gives us the language to understand them in day-to-day life . " <p> Among the practical examples Judaism offers , Offman pointed to Shabbat . Instead of seeing candles , challah , wine and Havdalah only as ritual obligations , Offman sees them as tools for integration -- practices that ask people to slow down , become present and bring insight into time , body and community . <p> She describes the dissolution of self that psychedelic experiences can invoke as deeply aligned with the Kabbalistic philosophy of " oneness " -- the idea that all things are connected . <p> Although these ideas of connectivity can be intellectually understood without psychedelics , Offman said the use of specific substances can bring a deeper emotional awareness to them . <p> A room of conversation <p> In early June , Toronto lawyer Marc Goldgrub gathered people in a room above Kensington Market to discuss Jews and psychedelics . The room was small enough that he capped attendance at 20 . <p> Goldgrub began with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Then participants broke into rotating discussion groups to talk about their experiences with Jewish life , psychedelics , mysticism and healing . At the end , they did a Jewish meditation based on the work of Aryeh Kaplan , a rabbi and author whose book Jewish Meditation helped popularize Jewish contemplative practice for modern readers . Afterward , people drank tea , exchanged numbers and joined a Canadian Jewish psychedelic WhatsApp group . <p> For Goldgrub , the most striking reaction was recognition . <p> " A lot of the people who showed up kind of had the sentiment of , ' I did n't realize that there was a psychedelic Jewish community . I did n't realize there was a connection between these two things , ' " he said . <p> Goldgrub is the founder of Green Economy Law , a Toronto firm that works with environmental businesses and psychedelic clients . He also created PsychedelicLaw.ca , an online resource that explains the rules around substances such as psilocybin , MDMA , ayahuasca , DMT , ketamine and ibogaine . <p> He came to psychedelic law in 2021 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in Canada and clinical research was turning to studies of psychedelics for post-traumatic stress disorder , depression , addiction and end-of-life distress . <p> The Jewish piece came later . Goldgrub had long been interested in psychedelics and Jewish culture . He had served in the Israel Defense Forces , studied Kabbalah at Hebrew University and visited Tzfat , the northern Israeli city associated with Jewish mysticism . But it was Madison Margolin 's book Exile and Ecstasy that helped him see Jewish psychedelics not merely as a curiosity , but as a vibrant world that exists beneath the surface . <p> " I did n't realize that there was a whole world of psychedelic Jews , " he said -- Jews combining mysticism , psychedelics , hippie culture , Jewish Renewal and Kabbalah . <p> That world , he said , is more organized outside Canada . Shefa , a U.S.-based organization whose name means " flow " or " abundance , " offers Jewish psychedelic education , integration circles , retreats , a podcast and a rabbinic cohort . The Jewish Healing Society , an online network and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ across what it describes as a transnational Jewish psychedelic community . Other projects , including an international retreat organization called Ripples , have explored ayahuasca ceremonies with Israelis and Palestinians after Oct. 7 . <p> " It 's not just that there 's a bunch of Jews in psychedelics . It 's that they 're organized , " Goldgrub said . <p> Izzy Waxman , a Toronto educator and activist who taught a course on psychedelic Jewish mysticism , sees the same pattern . <p> Waxman taught " Riding the Chariot : Psychedelic Jewish Mysticism and the Fringes of Consciousness " through Lishma , a Jewish learning program in Toronto for people in their 20s and 30s . <p> The program itself grew partly out of Waxman 's frustration . Years ago , at a panel in which Waxman discussed barriers to Jewish engagement for young adults , she said too many Jewish education offerings felt overly simplistic when it came to ushering young Jews into spiritual practice , describing events such as " drinks and dreidels , " or " vodka and latkes . " <p> " If I @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ bar in the city , " she said . " But I want to study Kabbalah , and this was not made available . " <p> Eventually , she said , she found her own path toward studying Kabbalah , and a few Toronto synagogues came together to offer her space to teach the course she had once been searching for . <p> Her course explored the overlap between psychedelic experience and Jewish mystical traditions . Waxman is careful not to reduce Judaism to psychedelic language , or psychedelics to Jewish theology . But she sees a real conversation between the two . <p> Psychedelics , she said , can help Jews who have experienced Judaism as rote , flat or purely intellectual , access emotional and mystical dimensions of texts and prayers . <p> " I think psychedelics can open Jewish people , who have never had that kind of spiritual experience , to a really rich emotional , experiential , mystical experience that they can bring back to inform their Judaism , " she said . " You come back and can read the same texts and the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . " <p> At the same time , she believes Judaism has something essential to offer psychedelics : caution , structure and grounding . <p> " I think mysticism and psychedelics should kind of come with the same warnings on the tin , " Waxman said . <p> Jewish tradition has long treated mysticism as powerful but potentially dangerous , with warnings about who should study it , when and how . Waxman believes the psychedelic world needs similar humility . <p> " There are risks . There are real risks . People are harmed , " she said . " There are safer and less safe ways to engage , and there is also no way to engage that is 100 per cent safe . " <p> That caution runs through Jewish thought , she said , because Judaism is not ultimately about chasing private ecstasy . It is about practice , obligation , community and ethical life . <p> Psychedelic experiences may leave people feeling that everything is connected and sacred . But without integration , Waxman said , those feelings can become ungrounded or grandiose . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ early 1960s , LSD and other psychedelics were studied in therapeutic and scientific settings , including for alcoholism , anxiety and depression . But as psychedelics became symbols of the counterculture , the research became politically and culturally radioactive . Beneath that countercultural surface , in the 1950s , the CIA , with funding from the Canadian government , deployed abusive mind-control experiments that are still facing legal consequences today . <p> By the early 1970s , strict drug laws and regulatory barriers had largely halted the first wave of psychedelic research . In recent years , however , a new wave of more carefully regulated clinical studies has reported promising results for some patients , including reductions in symptoms of depression and post-traumatic stress disorder , and relief from end-of-life anxiety in patients with serious illness . <p> Pollan and other chroniclers of the current psychedelic renaissance have argued that the contemporary field has tried to distance itself from earlier uses by emphasizing clinical structure : screening , preparation , trained guides , controlled settings and integration afterward . <p> Waxman said Jewish tradition may offer not only mystical @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ experiences back into ordinary life through structure and discourse . <p> " It 's not about chasing the spiritual highs , " she said . " Come back and use those gifts , integrate it into your life , into the life you 're already living . " <p> Personally , Waxman no longer uses psychedelics , although she did when she was younger . The most important thing they did , she said , was bring her to Jewish mysticism . <p> " The switch has been turned on and it can not be turned off , " she said . " I just do n't need it anymore . " <p> Complementing Jewish ritual <p> Two Jews interviewed by The Canadian Jewish News requested anonymity because of the legal status of substances such as LSD and psilocybin in Canada . One young professional said magic mushrooms helped them connect emotionally with Jewish ideas they had previously understood only intellectually . <p> " The use of magic mushrooms gave me this deeper sense of spirituality I feel has been missing in standard Jewish education , " they said . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 've learned in my Jewish upbringing , but they 've allowed me to connect with those ideas on a more immediate level . " <p> Another young Canadian , who said they wrap tefillin daily , described microdosing psilocybin at roughly 100 milligrams every two days . <p> " At this dose , there is n't really any sensory distortion . You do n't get any weird visuals , or anything like that , " they said . " But there is a deeper presence and introspection that complement prayer and Jewish ritual . " <p> Roda , an anthropologist and ethnomusicologist , came to the subject through her work on Orthodox Jewish women , music , art , wellness and healing . Her book For Women and Girls Only : Reshaping Jewish Orthodoxy Through the Arts in the Digital Age , examined how ultra-Orthodox and formerly ultra-Orthodox women used media , performance and women-only artistic spaces to reshape religious life . Her newer research looks at altered states of consciousness , breathwork and psychedelics . <p> Roda said the use of psychedelics among Hasidic and ex-Hasidic Jews is less @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of healing " emerging around Orthodox life . Some participants , she said , first encountered ayahuasca or other psychedelic practices outside explicitly Jewish circles , before adapting those experiences through Jewish music , prayer , culture or ritual . <p> She connects the interest to a broader search for healing inside Orthodox communities , including mental-health struggles , a crisis of religious authority and a desire for spiritual experience beyond existing institutional structures . <p> ' The medicine is a vehicle ' <p> Jivian Dicovski , an Israeli facilitator and guide for psychedelic ceremonies , has repeatedly seen the potential for psychedelic healing in Jewish circles up close . <p> He is collaborating on a project he says is being developed for the Jewish community in Vancouver , involving microdosing and macrodosing ayahuasca , a psychoactive plant brew traditionally associated with Amazonian Indigenous and syncretic religious practices . He has recently worked with Hasidic Jews and says he sees rising interest in the psychedelic -- or , what he calls " medicine " -- world among Jewish communities , particularly in Israel after Oct. 7 . Before becoming a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in the corporate world , then left as his own search for healing and purpose deepened . <p> " For me , medicine is a tool . It 's a vehicle , " he said . " The medicine is not the important thing here . The most important thing is where you get with the medicine . " <p> Dicovski says he has guided hundreds of ceremonies and about 65 microdosing groups . He is cautious about sharing participant stories , citing confidentiality . But he speaks openly about what makes the work possible . <p> " The prerequisite for everything that you do , to any journey you go within yourself , is about safety , " he said . " If you can relax , you can go much deeper within yourself . And if you can not relax , it 's a problem . You have to keep one eye open and see that you 're being safe . " <p> Since Oct. 7 , he said , more Jews have wanted to sit in ceremony with other Jews . He stressed that he also works @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is rooted in peace . But ceremony , he said , requires boundaries . <p> In discussing the Vancouver project , he said some people asked whether anti-Zionist participants could come wearing keffiyehs . His answer was no -- not because emotions about Israel , Gaza or war are unwelcome , but because political declarations can destabilize the experience . <p> " You 're most welcome to bring your feelings , " he said . " If you have emotions about that , please . " <p> But , he said , ceremony is not a place for agendas . <p> " Agendas are the killer of going in . " <p> Dicovski also warns against treating psychedelics as a cure-all . <p> " It 's not like a magic pill that will happen and you take it and all your problems will be solved , " he said . " It 's how you continue working with it . " <p> The risks and the law <p> In Canada , the legal landscape adds another layer of caution . Health Canada says activities with magic mushrooms , psilocybin and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ illegal unless authorized by Health Canada . Requests involving psilocybin and MDMA may be made through the Special Access Program , but authorization is assessed case by case and is not guaranteed . <p> A wider concern expressed by some practitioners is not that classic psychedelics are known to be physically addictive in the same way as opioids or stimulants ( the majority of research says they are not ) , but that powerful altered states can be psychologically disruptive , especially without screening , support and integration . Some worry that a person who experiences dramatic insight or relief may keep seeking stronger or more frequent experiences instead of doing the slower work of therapy , ritual and self-examination . <p> The work after the door opens <p> In Offman 's practice , the psychedelic session is not treated as a revelation that stands on its own . It is one point in a longer relationship . Some clients have worked with her for years before adding psychedelic sessions . Others return afterward to understand what surfaced , how it connects to their lives and what they are supposed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the gas chamber , Offman says she 's " found herself closer to her faith . " <p> " That 's why I think psychedelics are emerging so prominently in Jewish life . It 's a reconnection after October 7 . Because in the face of such horrors we continue to find love and meaning and purpose in the multitude of ways of being Jewish . " <p> Offman said she often works with non-Jewish clients , but her own Jewishness is not absent from the room . She uses personal disclosure when appropriate and speaks openly with clients about how she understands the world as a Jew . " Take what you want and need from that to understand for yourself , " she tells her patients . <p> That Jewish context is not only about trauma , she says . It is also about what she believes Jewish life can still offer in a fractured moment . <p> " This oneness is at the root of Judaism and it is the antidote for our suffering at this moment , " she said . " We are fractured , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ joy in our community that if we begin to heal ourselves and as a community , then there 's a really good chance that we can heal the world as well . " <h> Author <p> Mitch is The CJN 's campus and education reporter based in Toronto , Ont . He has a passion for investigative research , long-form feature writing and digital journalism . @@139318952 <h> International news <p> 1 . U.S. -- Iran pause lowers immediate pressure on oil prices <p> The United States halted its bombing campaign against Iran after 13 nights , while Tehran indicated it would pause attacks as long as the American pause holds . Iran continues to say it controls the Strait of Hormuz and has not agreed to resume negotiations . Brent crude fell sharply Monday , dropping below $90 US a barrel during morning trading . <p> Why it matters locally : The Strait of Hormuz is a critical global oil route . Continued de-escalation could reduce pressure on fuel , aviation and freight costs , although changes at Northwestern Ontario gas stations would not necessarily be immediate . Renewed attacks could quickly reverse the decline , raising costs for trucking , mining exploration , forestry operations and municipal fleets . <p> 2 . Israel approves framework for an international Gaza force <p> Israel says it will allow a multinational International Stabilization Force into the Gaza Strip under the current ceasefire framework . About 200 personnel from countries including Uganda and Morocco could participate , but Israel @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has not been established , and major elements of the broader peace plan remain unresolved . <p> Why it matters locally : The development will be closely followed by Palestinian , Israeli , Muslim and Jewish communities in Canada , as well as humanitarian organizations . It could also influence Canada 's diplomatic position and future decisions involving aid , reconstruction and international peace operations . <p> 3 . Trump and Zelenskyy prepare for Washington meeting <p> U.S. President Donald Trump and Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy are scheduled to meet Tuesday in Washington . American and Ukrainian officials have discussed a possible air ceasefire proposal , although the Kremlin said Monday it had not received a tangible new peace proposal . <p> Why it matters locally : Northwestern Ontario has longstanding Ukrainian community connections . Northwestern Ontario faces similar resilience questions involving highways , rail lines , border services and the movement of critical minerals and forest products . <p> 3 . Mexico was also preparing to send additional firefighters to Canada . <h> Regional news : Northwestern Ontario <p> 1 . Northwest Fire Region @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ regional report listed 130 active fires as of Saturday evening . Twenty-seven were not under control , five were being held , seven were under control and 91 were being observed . Suppression work was underway near Thunder Bay , Atikokan , Cat Lake First Nation and the Brightsand River area . Thunder Bay 64 , near Wabinosha Bay and about 12 kilometres east of Highway 527 , remained out of control . <p> Readers should expect those numbers and classifications to change throughout Monday . U.S. mineral-supply deadline creates an opening for Northern Ontario <p> The United States is preparing to restrict government purchases of several critical minerals and rare-earth products sourced from China , Russia , Iran and North Korea beginning Jan. 1 , 2027 . American production remains far below projected demand , particularly for rare-earth magnets . <p> That gap could strengthen the strategic case for Northwestern Ontario mining and processing projects . A provincial investment is already supporting research into whether a Thunder Bay-area pulp byproduct could be used by Rock Tech Lithium 's proposed Red Rock processing facility . <p> The opportunity is significant but not automatic . New mines and processing plants still require financing , transportation and electricity infrastructure , environmental review and meaningful agreements with affected Indigenous nations . <h> Local news : Thunder Bay <p> 1 . New statistics require careful crime coverage <p> Thunder Bay 's police-reported crime rate rose 18 per cent in 2025 to 8,373 incidents per 100,000 people , the largest percentage increase among the census metropolitan areas included in the Statistics Canada comparison . The city 's overall Crime Severity Index rose three per cent to 114.1 , while national conventional crime rates and severity declined . <p> The crime rate measures the number of reported incidents , while the Crime Severity Index gives greater weight to more serious offences . Reporting should avoid treating the two measures as interchangeable and should examine which offence categories drove the increase . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The Northwestern Ontario wildfire emergency should lead , particularly evacuation information and direct reporting from affected First Nations . The Canada -- U.S. tariff deadline is the strongest economic story because of its implications for forestry , mining and freight . Thunder Bay fog and highway visibility should receive prominent morning placement as immediate public-safety information . <p> The U.S. -- Iran pause and falling oil prices deserve an international lead with a practical regional cost-of-living angle . @@139377949 <p> For her 97th birthday , Frances Belzberg had a request . She wanted to try something she had n't yet : skydiving . She had a blast . <p> When she turned 98 in July , 2025 , she ziplined at Vancouver 's Grouse Mountain . In order to be eligible , staff informed her , she needed to be able to raise her legs . Could she ? Oh , yes , she could , and then some . Pilates . <p> This is the spirit in which Ms. Belzberg , Fran to all , lived : embracing every opportunity , every human being , life -- to the fullest . She wanted the best for her family , for the people in her circle , including her caregivers -- and the world . She lived with exuberance , empathy , humour , wisdom and spirit ; withan open heart and an open wallet . <p> A notable philanthropist , she was named tothe Order of Canada in 1995 and the Order of British Columbia in 2021 . Ms. Belzberg , along with her late husband , Samuel @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ numerous causes , establishing the Dystonia Medical Research Foundation and helping to create the Simon Wiesenthal Center Museum of Tolerance in Los Angeles , where shewas the founding chair . This was one of many Jewish causes they supported . They also provided funding for AIDS research ; generously supportedthe University of British Columbia , Simon Fraser University and BC Children 's Hospital ; and partnered with the federal government to establish Action Canada , which provides fellowships for potential future policy-makers . <p> " Fran 's loss is the family 's loss first , but it is also Canada 's loss , " Burnaby North-Seymour MP and Action Canada alumnus Terry Beech said Sunday at her funeral , where he also delivered formal condolences from the Prime Minister . <p> Ms. Belzberg had been raised in Los Angeles under difficult circumstances , in an immigrant family that had cheated death . She never lost her work ethic or forgot her humble beginnings -- even as the dynasty she and her husband built allowed her beautiful homes in Vancouver 's Shaughnessy neighbourhood and in Palm Springs , Calif. , trips @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ no longer safe for her to be behind the wheel , Fran missed driving so desperately that she stole the keys and took her car out for one last joyride , the desert wind in her blond hair . <p> But she had hardships . When her oldest daughter , Cheri , started experiencing mysterious uncontrollable leg movements and later lost her ability to speak , the Belzbergs spent two years trying to figure out what was wrong . Some doctors suggested this was the result of hysteria , brought on by her decision to become religiously observant . When she was finally diagnosed with a rare form of the neurological movement disorder dystonia , the Belzbergs established a foundation to conduct research and provide support . <p> " Mum said , ' I 'm never going to let another family go through that , ' " her youngest daughter , Lisa Belzberg , said in an interview . <p> In 1976 , the Belzbergs co-founded the Dystonia Medical Research Foundation . She had planned to be at the next meeting in August . <p> At home , she was the fun mom -- taking her kids out early from school to go skiing at Grouse and , later , making each and every grandchild feel as if they were her favourite . <p> " She was n't just open to adventure , she was often leading the charge , " her grandson Cooper Zelnick said at her funeral . He also recalled his grandmother 's 96th-birthday surprise for her shocked luncheon guests : male strippers . " Thank God she did n't make it to 100 , " he joked . " I have no idea what she would have done . " Please let me go . " <p> Frances was born July 10 , 1927 , in Los Angeles to David and Dena Cooper , who had left Radom , Poland , in 1923 . David died when Fran was only 17 . Dena scraped together the money to buy some small apartment buildings , and Fran and her brothers Bert , Harry and Ben were enlisted to collect rent , fix things for tenants and clean out empty suites . <p> As a teenager , Fran volunteered to work with new immigrants from Mexico and helped people from marginalized communities learn to read and write English . Courtesy of family <p> Fran and Sam married in 1950 , and Fran arrived in Edmonton on a very cold November day . It was difficult and lonely at first , but she quickly became active in the Jewish community . " She went from licking envelopes at Hadassah to running Hadassah to running around the country speaking for Hadassah , " Lisa said . <p> In Edmonton , they had four children : Cheri , Marc , Wendy and Lisa . <p> Finally , with business going very well , Sam @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Vancouver . She picked the latter , where she could ski , play tennis , be outside . They moved in 1967 . <p> Fran sat on many boards and became involved in causes that spoke to her , including the Vancouver Art Gallery . She supportedits " take the gallery to court " campaign that would see the VAG move from its original space to its current location , a renovated courthouse . <p> She was an art lover herself , collecting the Canadian worksthat hung in her husband 's offices . On her own walls , Jack Shadbolt and Dorothy Knowles were particular favourites . <p> Lisa recalled buying jeans at the mall with her mother in 1985 when the woman at the counter recognized the name on her mother 's credit card . The woman then recounted how years earlier , she had cold-called Fran , looking for advice about opening her own art gallery . Fran hadspent an hour on the phone with her , telling her to follow her dreams -- which she did ( she was working retail part-time to supplement her gallery income @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ me the confidence to try something I 've never tried before , " she told Lisa . " I just dreamed that I 'd meet her one day . " <p> Sam died in 2018 , after suffering a stroke while working at his desk . At his funeral , Fran offered input to speakers as they eulogized her husband , interjecting with comments such as " that 's enough , sit down , and stop crying , " Mr. Zelnick recalled at her funeral . <p> After that , she gave up the house for an ocean-view condo in Vancouver 's Coal Harbour . She loved to look out as the seaplanes took off and landed . <p> In her last years , Ms. Belzberg was shaken by the antisemitism that followed the attacks on Israel by Hamas on Oct. 7 , 2023 , and Israel 's resulting war in Gaza . <p> After Oct. 7 , the sign at SFU Vancouver 's Samuel and Frances Belzberg Library at the Morris J. Wosk Centre for Dialogue was defaced . In a newspaper editorial , Ms. Belzberg expressed hope , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . " Even in my grief and outrage , I am convinced there is much to discuss , much to learn , on the road to recovery and , God willing , peace and reconciliation . " <p> Frances Belzberg leaves her four children , 25 grandchildren and 37 great-grandchildren ( plus two on the way ) , the youngest of whom , Miles , was born July 9 , the day before Fran turned 99 . Family flew in from all over the world to be by her side . <p> " She had an amazing , amazing life , " Mr. Zelnick said in an interview . " To make it to 99 and be surrounded by family , it 's not so bad . "
